BBN PDP-11 / tcp/psipout.c
SHA-256 716ad708d0302e1eb0afe31959f432c10692e403860244b2fba6ba1164063f93

    1  #
    2  # include "tcpstru.h"
    3  # include "hdrmasks.h"
    4  
    5  # define ENDARGS        -1
    6  
    7  
    8  PsipOut(WrkPtr)
    9  
   10  struct WorkBlk *WrkPtr;
   11  
   12  {
   13     extern char FlkBuff[TCPOTSZ];
   14     extern int FlkFlag;
   15     extern int Auth;
   16     extern int Debug;
   17     extern struct FdsBlk FdsBffer[FDSSIZE];
   18     extern char TcpOut[TCPOTSZ];
   19     extern int SegData;
   20     extern int PartWr;
   21     extern int NetFdsW;
   22     extern char *OutPtr;
   23     extern int OutCnt;
   24     extern ANLngth;
   25  
   26     register struct TcpTcb *XPtr;
   27     register char *DPtr;
   28     register char *SPtr;
   29  
   30     int DataSent;
   31     char *FPtr;
   32     int RemData;
   33     char *EndPtr;
   34     int i,j;
   35     int R1;
   36     int Beyond;
   37     struct FdsBlk *FdsBffPtr;
   38     int SecChng;
   39     int SegSec;
   40     int Count;
   41  
   42     struct
   43     {  int ADat;
   44      } *APtr;
   45  
   46  
   47     if (Debug == 1)
   48     {  printf("PsipOut: entered\n");
   49      }
   50  
   51     /* check if part of previous segment needs to be written */
   52  
   53     if (PartWr == 1)
   54     {  /* still have some of buffer left to export */
   55        Count = write(NetFdsW, OutPtr, OutCnt);
   56        if (Count == -1)
   57        {  printf("PsipOut: write error\n");
   58           return(1);     /* leave on work queue */
   59         }
   60  
   61        if (Count != OutCnt)
   62        {  OutPtr =+ Count;
   63           OutCnt =- Count;
   64           if (Debug == 4)
   65           {  Log(4, "s", "Part write", -1);
   66            }
   67           return(1);     /* leave on work queue */
   68         }
   69  
   70        PartWr = 0;
   71      }
   72  
   73     XPtr = WrkPtr -> TCBPtr;
   74  
   75     if (XPtr == &FlkFlag)
   76     {  /* send duplicate or out of order segment */
   77        /* copy segment to TcpOut buffer because
   78        of partial write problem with raw message interface */
   79  
   80        SPtr = &FlkBuff[BSLLNGTH - ANLngth*2 - 2];
   81        DPtr = &TcpOut[BSLLNGTH - ANLngth*2 - 2];
   82  
   83        APtr = SPtr;
   84  
   85        OutPtr = DPtr;
   86        OutCnt = APtr -> ADat;
   87  
   88        for (i = 0; i < OutCnt; i++)
   89        {  *DPtr++ = *SPtr++;
   90         }
   91  
   92        FlkFlag = 0;
   93  
   94        Count = write(NetFdsW, OutPtr, OutCnt);
   95  
   96        if (Count == -1)
   97        {  printf("PsipOut: write error\n");
   98           PartWr = 1;
   99           return(1);    /* leave on work queue */
  100         }
  101  
  102        if (Count != OutCnt)
  103        {  OutPtr =+ Count;
  104           OutCnt =- Count;
  105           PartWr = 1;
  106           return(1);    /* leave on work queue */
  107         }
  108        return(0);
  109      }
  110  
  111     if (((XPtr -> Flags)&040) > 0)
  112     {  /* RST received - send no more segments */
  113        return(0);    /* remove work block */
  114      }
  115  
  116     /* send out a segment */
  117  
  118     XPtr -> SeqNo = XPtr -> SendSeq;
  119  
  120     i = 0;   /* byte counter */
  121  
  122     Beyond = 0;    /* flag: 1 => sending beyond right edge */
  123  
  124     /* calculate remaining data to be sent */
  125     RemData = (XPtr -> DataTl) - (XPtr -> DataNxt);
  126  
  127     if (RemData < 0)
  128     {  RemData =+ RBUFFSIZE;
  129      }
  130  
  131     /* check if SYN needs to be sent */
  132     if (XPtr -> SeqNo == XPtr -> SndISN)
  133     {  /* first sequence number */
  134        XPtr -> TCPFlags =| SYN;    /* set SYN bit */
  135        XPtr -> SendSeq =+ 1;
  136  
  137        if (XPtr -> State == OPEN)
  138        {  XPtr -> State = SYNSENT;
  139         }
  140      }
  141  
  142     /* check for urgent state */
  143     if (((XPtr -> Flags)&01) > 0)
  144     {  /* urgent condition flag on */
  145        /* check if urgent number is beyond next to send */
  146  
  147        R1 = ULCompare(&(XPtr -> SndUrgNo), &(XPtr -> SeqNo));
  148  
  149        if (R1 != 2)
  150        /* SndUrgNo >= SendSeq */
  151        {  /* put on the urgent bit */
  152           XPtr -> UrgentPtr = (XPtr -> SndUrgNo) - (XPtr -> SeqNo);
  153           XPtr -> TCPFlags =| URG;    /* URG <- 1 */
  154         }
  155      }
  156  
  157     DataSent = (XPtr -> DataNxt) - (XPtr -> DataHd);
  158  
  159     if (DataSent < 0)
  160     {  DataSent =+ RBUFFSIZE;
  161      }
  162  
  163     if (RemData != 0)
  164     {
  165        DPtr = &TcpOut[BSLLNGTH + INLNGTH + TCPLNGTH];
  166        SPtr = &(XPtr -> RetranBuff[XPtr -> DataNxt]);
  167        FPtr = &(XPtr -> FlagBuff[XPtr -> DataNxt]);
  168        EndPtr = &(XPtr -> RetranBuff[RBUFFSIZE]);
  169  
  170        /* pick up the security level */
  171        SegSec = (*FPtr) >> 3;
  172  
  173        /* check if being held up by window limits */
  174        if ((XPtr -> SndWindow == 0)||
  175        ((((XPtr -> TCPFlags)&URG) > 0)&&(DataSent >= XPtr -> SndWindow)))
  176  
  177        {  /* check if this is the first segment into a zero window or
  178           that this segment is a retransmission */
  179  
  180           if ((((XPtr -> Flags)&XRTNDATA) == 0)||
  181           (((XPtr -> Flags)&(XRTNDATA+XRETRANOK)) == (XRTNDATA+XRETRANOK)))
  182           {  /* send at least one byte */
  183              *DPtr = *SPtr;
  184              i = 1;
  185              /* prohibit data sent until retransmission time */
  186              /* this could occur with an ACK being sent */
  187  
  188              XPtr -> Flags =| XRTNDATA;
  189            }
  190  
  191           /* ensure that no more data is sent until retransmission */
  192           Beyond = 1;
  193         }
  194        else
  195        {
  196           SecChng = 0;
  197           while ( ((i + DataSent) < (XPtr -> SndWindow))&&
  198           (i < RemData)&&( i < SegData)&&(SecChng == 0) )
  199           {  *DPtr++ = *SPtr++;
  200              i++;
  201              if (SPtr == EndPtr)
  202              {  SPtr = &(XPtr -> RetranBuff[0]);
  203                 FPtr = &(XPtr -> FlagBuff[0]);
  204               }
  205              if (Auth == 1)
  206              {  if (((*FPtr) >> 3) == SegSec)
  207                 {  FPtr++;
  208                  }
  209                 else
  210                 {  SecChng = 1;
  211                  }
  212               }
  213            }
  214         }
  215      }
  216  
  217     /* reset the retran flag */
  218     XPtr -> Flags =& ~XRETRANOK;
  219  
  220     /* check if time to send FIN */
  221     if ((((XPtr -> Flags)&010) > 0)&&
  222     (XPtr -> SndFSN == XPtr -> SendSeq))
  223     {  /* no more data will be sent */
  224        XPtr -> TCPFlags =| FIN;    /* FIN <- 1 */
  225      }
  226  
  227     if ((i == 0)&&(((XPtr -> TCPFlags)&0377) == 0))
  228     {  /* nothing to send */
  229        if (RemData > 0)
  230        {  /* still have data though */
  231           return(1);
  232         }
  233        return(0);
  234      }
  235  
  236     /* set EOL bit always for the tenex guys */
  237  
  238     XPtr -> TCPFlags =| EOL;
  239  
  240     XPtr -> SegLength = i + INLNGTH + TCPLNGTH;
  241     /* always set ACK bit if connection is not SYNSENT */
  242     switch(XPtr -> State)
  243     {  case SYNRECD:
  244        case ESTAB:
  245        case CLOSEWAIT:
  246        case FINWAIT:
  247        case CLOSING:
  248        {  /* set ACK bit */
  249           XPtr -> TCPFlags =| ACK;
  250         }
  251      }
  252  
  253     /* set the security level */
  254     if (i == 0)   /* check for data */
  255     {  XPtr -> Options[2] = XPtr -> ScMinOut;
  256      }
  257     else
  258     {  XPtr -> Options[2] = SegSec;
  259      }
  260  
  261     /* set precedence level */
  262     XPtr -> Options[2] =| (XPtr -> SndPrec) << 4;
  263  
  264     /* set the TCC */
  265     XPtr -> Options[3] = XPtr -> TccList[0];
  266  
  267     /* copy leaders to output buffer */
  268  
  269     DPtr = TcpOut;
  270     SPtr = &(XPtr -> PrecSec);
  271  
  272     for (j = 0; j < (BSLLNGTH + INLNGTH + TCPLNGTH); j++)
  273     {  *DPtr++ = *SPtr++;
  274      }
  275  
  276     /* write out the segment to the net */
  277  
  278     NetOut(0);
  279  
  280     /* update the state information */
  281     XPtr -> DataNxt =+ i;
  282  
  283     if ((XPtr -> DataNxt) >= RBUFFSIZE)
  284     {  XPtr -> DataNxt =- RBUFFSIZE;
  285      }
  286  
  287     XPtr -> SendSeq =+ i;
  288  
  289     if (Debug == 1)
  290     {  /* snap the segment info */
  291        printf("PsipOut: segment info:  L = %d, F = %o, Src = %d\n", i,
  292        (XPtr -> TCPFlags)&07777, XPtr -> SrcePort);
  293      }
  294  
  295     if ((((XPtr -> TCPFlags)&RST) > 0)&&(((XPtr -> Flags)&020) > 0))
  296     {  /* the RST was sent, now clean up the connection */
  297        return(3);    /* make the scheduler abort the connection */
  298      }
  299  
  300     /* check for state change */
  301     if (((XPtr -> TCPFlags)&FIN) > 0)
  302     {  XPtr -> SendSeq =+ 1;
  303  
  304        switch(XPtr -> State)
  305        {  case SYNRECD:
  306           case ESTAB:
  307           {  XPtr -> State = FINWAIT;
  308              break;
  309            }
  310           case CLOSEWAIT:
  311           {  XPtr -> State = CLOSING;
  312              break;
  313            }
  314         }
  315      }
  316  
  317     /* update MaxSend if not retransmitting */
  318     R1 = ULCompare(&(XPtr -> SendSeq),&(XPtr -> MaxSend));
  319  
  320     if (R1 == 1)
  321     /* SendSeq > MaxSend */
  322     {  XPtr -> MaxSend = XPtr -> SendSeq;
  323      }
  324  
  325     if ((i > 0)||(((XPtr -> TCPFlags)&(SYN+FIN)) > 0))
  326     {  /* put TCB on retransmission queue */
  327        QTcb(XPtr);
  328      }
  329  
  330     /* clear the control bits */
  331     XPtr -> TCPFlags =& ~07777;
  332  
  333     /* if window not closed,
  334     move work block to end of queue if
  335     there is more data to segmentize */
  336     if ((Beyond != 1)&&((RemData - i) > 0))
  337     {
  338        return(2);   /* move to end of queue */
  339      }
  340     if ((Beyond == 1)&&(PartWr == 1))
  341     {  printf("B = 1 and PWr = 1\n");
  342      }
  343  
  344     if (PartWr == 1)
  345     {  return(1);      /* leave on queue */
  346      }
  347  
  348     return(0);   /* delete work entry */
  349   }
  350  
  351  
  352  
  353  NetOut(EchoSeg)
  354  
  355  int EchoSeg;
  356  
  357  {
  358     extern int ShortCkt;
  359     extern int ShortIn;
  360     extern int PartWr;
  361     extern char *OutPtr;
  362     extern int OutCnt;
  363     extern int *TimePtr;
  364     extern int RTime;
  365     extern int FlkFlag;
  366     extern char FlkBuff[TCPOTSZ];
  367     extern int SegNum;
  368     extern int ANLngth;
  369     extern int Debug;
  370     extern int InLink;
  371     extern int LocHost;
  372     extern int LocImp;
  373     extern int LocNet;
  374     extern int NetFdsW;
  375     extern int NetFdsR;
  376     extern char TcpOut[TCPOTSZ];
  377     extern char TcpIn[TCPINSZ];
  378     extern char NumLost;
  379     extern char NumBad;
  380     extern char NumDup;
  381     extern char TimeDup;
  382     extern char NumReOrd;
  383     extern char TimeReOrd;
  384     extern int NetTable[NUMNET];
  385     extern int DoChkSum;
  386  
  387     register char *SPtr, *DPtr;
  388  
  389     struct ANMask1 *ANPtr1;
  390     struct ANMask2 *ANPtr2;
  391     struct INLMask *INPtr;
  392     struct TCPMask *TCPPtr;
  393  
  394     int Error;
  395     int ILength;
  396     int DLength;
  397     int TLength;
  398     int Count;
  399     char *BPtr;
  400     int Chk;
  401     int Echo;
  402     int PHdr[2];
  403     int i;
  404  
  405     INPtr = &TcpOut[BSLLNGTH];
  406     ILength = ((INPtr -> IVerHdr)&017)*4;
  407  
  408     if (ShortCkt == 1)
  409     {  /* check if this segment is destined for this host */
  410  
  411        if ((INPtr -> IDstNet == LocNet)&&
  412        (INPtr -> IDHstH == LocHost)&&
  413        (((INPtr -> IDHstL)&0377) == LocImp))
  414        {  /* no real need to send to the net */
  415  
  416           /* set the segment ID */
  417           INPtr -> ISegID = SegNum;
  418           SegNum =+ 1;
  419  
  420           /* copy TcpOut buffer to TcpIn */
  421  
  422           SPtr = &TcpOut[BSLLNGTH];
  423           DPtr = &TcpIn[BSLLNGTH];
  424           Count = INPtr -> ISegLength;
  425  
  426           for (i = 0; i < Count; i++)
  427           {  *DPtr++ = *SPtr++;
  428            }
  429  
  430           ShortIn = 1;    /* set flag for PsipIn */
  431  
  432           /* make work for PsipIn */
  433  
  434           MakeWrk(0, NETINHP, 0, NetFdsR);
  435  
  436           return(0);
  437         }
  438      }
  439  
  440     ANPtr1 = &TcpOut[BSLLNGTH - ANLngth*2 - 2];
  441     ANPtr2 = ANPtr1;
  442  
  443     /* set the length regardless of AN leader size */
  444     ANPtr1 -> MLngth1 = ANLngth*2 + 2 + INPtr -> ISegLength;
  445  
  446     OutPtr = ANPtr1;
  447     OutCnt = ANPtr1 -> MLngth1;
  448  
  449     /* determine destination host */
  450  
  451     if (INPtr -> IDstNet >= NUMNET)
  452     {  i = NetTable[0];  /* send to smart gateway */
  453      }
  454     else
  455     {  i = NetTable[INPtr -> IDstNet];
  456      }
  457  
  458     if (i == -1)
  459     {  /* don't use gateway */
  460        i = ((INPtr -> IDHstL)&0377) + ((INPtr -> IDHstH) << 8);
  461      }
  462  
  463     if (ANLngth == 2)
  464     {  ANPtr1 -> Type1 = 0;
  465        ANPtr1 -> Link1 = InLink;    /* internet link number */
  466        ANPtr1 -> SbType1 = 0;
  467        ANPtr1 -> Host1 = (i&077) + ((i&01400) >> 2);
  468      }
  469     else
  470     {  ANPtr2 -> Fmt2 = 15;
  471        ANPtr2 -> SNet2 = 0;
  472        ANPtr2 -> LdrFlgs2 = 0;
  473        ANPtr2 -> Type2 = 0;
  474        ANPtr2 -> HandType2 = 0;
  475        ANPtr2 -> LogHost2 = 0;
  476        ANPtr2 -> Link2 = InLink;
  477        ANPtr2 -> SbType2 = 0;
  478        ANPtr2 -> Host2 = i >> 8;
  479        ANPtr2 -> Imp2 = i&0377;
  480  
  481        /* the next value may eventually have to be real */
  482        ANPtr2 -> ALngth2 = 0;
  483      }
  484  
  485     if (EchoSeg == 0)
  486     {  /* set the segment ID */
  487        INPtr -> ISegID = SegNum;
  488        SegNum =+ 1;
  489  
  490        TCPPtr = &TcpOut[BSLLNGTH + ILength];
  491        TLength = ((TCPPtr -> TTCPFlags) >> 12)*4;
  492  
  493        DLength = INPtr -> ISegLength - ILength - TLength;
  494  
  495        if (DoChkSum == 1)
  496        {  /* compute internet checksum */
  497           INPtr -> IINChkSum = ~ChkSum(INPtr, ILength/2, 0, 0);
  498  
  499           /* compute TCP checksum */
  500           /* point to internet source address */
  501  
  502           BPtr = &TcpOut[BSLLNGTH+12];
  503  
  504           Chk = ChkSum(BPtr, 4,0 ,0);
  505  
  506           BPtr = &PHdr[0];
  507           PHdr[0] = (INPtr -> ISegLength) - ILength;
  508           PHdr[1] = INPtr -> IProtocol;
  509  
  510           Chk = ChkSum(BPtr, 2, Chk, 0);
  511  
  512           BPtr = TCPPtr;
  513  
  514           Chk = ChkSum(BPtr, TLength/2, Chk, 0);
  515  
  516           BPtr = &TcpOut[BSLLNGTH + ILength + TLength];
  517  
  518           /* check for odd number of bytes */
  519           if ((DLength&01) > 0)
  520           {  /* need to pad with a zero byte */
  521  
  522              TcpOut[BSLLNGTH + ILength + TLength + DLength] = 0;
  523              DLength =+ 1;
  524            }
  525  
  526           TCPPtr -> TTCPChkSum = ~ChkSum(BPtr, DLength/2, Chk, 1);
  527         }
  528      }
  529  
  530     if ((Debug == 1)||(Debug == 2)||(Debug == 4))
  531     {
  532        Error = Log(1, "n", INPtr, ENDARGS);
  533        if (EchoSeg == 0)
  534        {  Error = Log(1, "t", TCPPtr, ENDARGS);
  535         }
  536      }
  537  
  538     /* swap bytes before sending */
  539  
  540     Swap(INPtr, ILength);
  541  
  542     if (EchoSeg == 0)
  543     {  Swap(TCPPtr, TLength);
  544      }
  545  
  546     /* flakiness simulation */
  547  
  548     /* do we need to lose the segment? */
  549     if (NumLost > 0)
  550     {  if (rand()%100 < NumLost)
  551        {  /* discard the segment */
  552           printf("Lost %d\n", SegNum-1);
  553           return(ANPtr1 -> MLngth1);  /* don't write to net */
  554         }
  555      }
  556  
  557     /* do we need to smash the segment? */
  558     if (NumBad > 0)
  559     {  if (rand()%100 < NumBad)
  560        {  i = BSLLNGTH + rand()%40;   /* 40 => in TCP/IN hdrs */
  561           TcpOut[i] = ~TcpOut[i];   /* some random place */
  562           printf("Smashed %d B: %d\n", SegNum-1, i);
  563         }
  564      }
  565  
  566     /* do we need to make a delayed duplicate? */
  567     if ((NumDup > 0)&&(FlkFlag == 0))
  568     {  if ((rand()%100) < NumDup)
  569        {  /* copy TcpOut to FlkBuff */
  570  
  571           SPtr = &TcpOut[BSLLNGTH - ANLngth*2 - 2];
  572           DPtr = &FlkBuff[BSLLNGTH - ANLngth*2 - 2];
  573  
  574           Count = ANPtr1 -> MLngth1;
  575  
  576           for (i = 0; i < Count; i++)
  577           {  *DPtr++ = *SPtr++;
  578            }
  579  
  580           RTime = (rand()%TimeDup) + *TimePtr;
  581  
  582           /* set flag to allow only one duplicate */
  583           FlkFlag = 1;
  584  
  585           printf("Duped %d T: %d\n", SegNum-1, RTime - *TimePtr);
  586         }
  587      }
  588  
  589     /* do we need to reorder the segment? */
  590     if ((NumReOrd > 0)&&(FlkFlag == 0))
  591     {  if ((rand()%100) < NumReOrd)
  592        {  /* copy TcpOut to FlkBuff */
  593  
  594           SPtr = &TcpOut[BSLLNGTH - ANLngth*2 - 2];
  595           DPtr = &FlkBuff[BSLLNGTH - ANLngth*2 - 2];
  596  
  597           Count = ANPtr1 -> MLngth1;
  598  
  599           for (i = 0; i < Count; i++)
  600           {  *DPtr++ = *SPtr++;
  601            }
  602  
  603           RTime = (rand()%TimeReOrd) + *TimePtr;
  604  
  605           /* set flag to allow only one duplicate */
  606           FlkFlag = 1;
  607  
  608           printf("Reordered %d T: %d\n", SegNum-1, RTime - *TimePtr);
  609  
  610           return(ANPtr1 -> MLngth1);  /* don't write to net */
  611         }
  612      }
  613  
  614     /* write the segment to the net */
  615  
  616     Count = write(NetFdsW, ANPtr1, ANPtr1 -> MLngth1);
  617  
  618     if (Count == -1)
  619     {  /* write error */
  620        printf("NetOut: write error\n");
  621        return(0);
  622      }
  623  
  624     if (Count != OutCnt)
  625     {  PartWr = 1;  /* need to finish sending this segment */
  626        OutPtr =+ Count;
  627        OutCnt =- Count;
  628        if (Debug == 4)
  629        {  Log(3, "s", "Part write", -1);
  630         }
  631      }
  632  
  633     return(0);
  634   }

  266  
  267  struct TcpTcb
  268  {  char ConnHandle;     /* local connection index */
  269     char State;          /* state of connection */
  270  
  271     /* see above for flag definitions */
  272     int Flags;           /* various work-to-do flags */
  273     char TMode;          /* mode of TcpOpen */
  274     char ProcID;         /* user ID */
  275     int TCPCmdPort;      /* file desc - TCP to user command port */
  276     int UserCmdPort;     /* file desc - user to TCP command port */
  277     int SendPort;        /* file desc - data from user port */
  278     int RecvPort;        /* file desc - data to user port */
  279  
  280     /* Open Parameters */
  281     char ScMaxOut;       /* max security user to net */
  282     char ScMinOut;       /* min security user to net */
  283     char ScMaxIn;        /* max security net to user */
  284     char ScMinIn;        /* min security net to user */
  285     char ATPrec;         /* maximum user precedence allowed */
  286     char SndPrec;        /* send precedence */
  287     char MnRcvPrec;      /* minimum receive precedence */
  288     char MxRcvPrec;      /* maximum receive precedence */
  289     char TCCCnt;         /* count of following TCCs */
  290     char TccList[NUMBTCC];  /* list of TCCs */
  291     char OpnNet;         /* foreign network */
  292     char OpnHstH;        /* foreign host - high byte */
  293     int OpnHstL;         /* foreign host - low word */
  294     int OpnPort;         /* foreign port */
  295  
  296     /* Binary Segment Leader */
  297     int PrecSec;         /* precedence, security fields */
  298     int TccCcc;          /* transmission control code, command control code */
  299     int SecTcc;          /* redundant security, TCC fields */
  300     int FnTcp;           /* foreign TCP ID */
  301     int VerSegNo;        /* version, s-segment number */
  302     int BSLSave[3];      /* unused BSL fields */
  303  
  304     /* InterNet Header */
  305     char TypeService;    /* type of service */
  306     char VerHdr;         /* 4 bit version, 4 bit header length */
  307     int SegLength;       /* length of segment in octets */
  308     int SegID;           /* segment identification */
  309     int FragOffset;      /* 3 bits flags, 13 bits fragment offset */
  310     char Protocol;       /* next layer protocol identifier */
  311     char TimeToLive;     /* time to live... */
  312     int INChkSum;        /* internet header checksum */
  313     char SrcHstH;        /* source host - high byte */
  314     char SrcNet;         /* source network */
  315     int SrcHstL;         /* source host - lower two bytes */
  316     char DstHstH;        /* destination host - high byte */
  317     char DstNet;         /* destination network */
  318     int DstHstL;         /* destination host - lower two bytes */
  319     char Options[4];     /* option field */
  320  
  321     /* TCP Header */
  322     int SrcePort;        /* source port */
  323     int DestPort;        /* destination port */
  324     long SeqNo;          /* sequence number */
  325     long AckNo;          /* acknowledge number */
  326     int TCPFlags;        /* 4 bits data offset, rest are flags */
  327     int Window;          /* flow control window */
  328     int TCPChkSum;       /* checksum of TCP & parts of IN header */
  329     int UrgentPtr;       /* urgent pointer */
  330  
  331     /* miscellaneous state information */
  332     long LeftSeq;        /* earliest unacknowledged octet seq no. */
  333     long SendSeq;        /* sequence # next to send */
  334     long MaxSend;        /* seq no. of last data sent + 1 */
  335     int DataHd;          /* octet last acknowledged */
  336     int DataNxt;         /* next octet to send */
  337     int DataTl;          /* last data item in send buffer */
  338     int SndWindow;       /* send window size in octets */
  339     char RetranTm;       /* retransmission time out */
  340     char NumRetran;      /* no. of retrans before good ACK */
  341     int RetrSw;          /* 1 => slow mode */
  342     long SndUrgNo;       /* urgent number for send */
  343     long RcvUrgNo;       /* urgent number for receive */
  344     int DataState;       /* state of data port from user */
  345     int DataLeft;        /* remaining data in input port */
  346     int LastAckTime;     /* time of last acknowledge - seconds */
  347     long SndISN;         /* initial send seq number */
  348     long RcvISN;         /* initial recv seq number */
  349     long SndFSN;         /* final send seq number */
  350     int SndSpc1;         /* data in send port */
  351     int RcvSpc1;         /* space in recv port */
  352  
  353     /* measurement data */
  354     int SegRecv;         /* # of segments recvd */
  355     int DupRecv;         /* # of duplicate segments received */
  356     int BusyRecv;        /* # of discarded segments */
  357     int RetranSeg;       /* # of retransmitted segments */
  358  
  359     /* Linkage area */
  360     int *NxtActTcb;      /* ptr to next active TCB */
  361     int *NxtRetran;      /* ptr to next retransmit TCB */
  362     int *LstRetran;      /* ptr to last retransmit TCB */
  363  
  364     /* Retransmission Ring Buffer */
  365     char RetranBuff[RBUFFSIZE];  /* input buffer/retransmission buffer */
  366     char FlagBuff[RBUFFSIZE];    /* flags associated with data */
  367  
  368   };
  369  
  370  struct WorkBlk        /* a block in the work list */
  371  {  char PortFlags;            /* misc work flags */
  372     char PortFds;              /* file descriptor of port */
  373     struct TcpTcb *TCBPtr;     /* ptr to TCB (also is fds for tcp) */
  374     struct WorkBlk *LastWork;  /* last node link address */
  375     struct WorkBlk *NextWork;  /* next node link address */
  376   };
  377  
  378  struct FdsBlk         /* picture of an FdsBffer entry */
  379  {  char BuffFlags;      /* misc work flags */
  380     char BuffFds;        /* fds for port */
  381     int *BuffTcbPtr;     /* ptr to relevent TCB, temp for fds usr cmd */
  382   };
  383  
  384  struct      /* used to break apart longs for printing */
  385  {  int bb[2];
  386   } *z;
  387  
  388  
  389  struct PortLdr
  390  {  int UsrPID;
  391     int GroupID;    /* file table pointer */
  392     char RealUserID;
  393     char EffUserID;
  394     int DCount;
  395   };

   69  
   70  1.  Introduction
   71  
   72  
   73       The TCP, or Transmission Control Protocol  [1],  provides  a
   74  
   75  facility for processes to communicate over the ARPANET, or across
   76  
   77  nets,  in  a  byte-stream  fashion.   This document describes the
   78  
   79  design overview and functional description of  an  implementation
   80  
   81  of the TCP for UNIX.
   82  
   83  
   84       Two  processes  on distinct hosts communicate by first using
   85  
   86  the TCP to establish a full-duplex stream-oriented  communication
   87  
   88  path  and  then using the TCP program to forward and receive data
   89  
   90  on their behalf.  Thus, the TCP is responsible for  handling  the
   91  
   92  mechanics of establishing and destroying the communication paths,
   93  
   94  packaging  the  stream  into  packets  for  network transmission,
   95  
   96  performing flow control,  and  coordinating  acknowledgments  for
   97  
   98  data successfully transferred.
   99  
  100  
  101          All TCP activity within UNIX is coordinated by  a  single
  102  
  103  process,  termed  the  TCP process.  The TCP process (1) accesses
  104                         ___ _______
  105  
  106  the network using a  device   driver   implemented   within   the
  107  
  108  UNIX   system,  (2)  implements   the   TCP   protocol,  and  (3)
  109  
  110  interfaces  to   application processes.  A collection of  library
  111  
  112  routines  which  implement the interface to the TCP process using
  113  
  114  UNIX  interprocess  communication  facilities  is  available  for
  115  
  116  application programs.
  117  
  118  
  119  
  120  
  121  
  122  
  123                                 -2-
  124  
  125  
  126  
  127  
  128  
  129  
  130  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
  131  
  132  
  133       An  application  process  is  termed  a  user.   All  of the
  134                                                ____
  135  
  136  interprocess communications used in this implementation are based
  137  
  138  on  the  RAND  port  mechanism[3]  with  some  additional   minor
  139  
  140  modifications  to  UNIX.   To  avoid  confusion  with  the use of
  141  
  142  "ports" in the TCP specification, the Rand  port  mechanism  will
  143  
  144  always be referenced as a "UNIX port".
  145  
  146  
  147       The  details  of  this design, the functional specifications
  148  
  149  for  the  library  routines,  and  the  extensions  to  the  UNIX
  150  
  151  operating   system  to  augment  the  interprocess  communication
  152  
  153  facilities are the subject of the rest of this document.
  154  
  155  
  156  
  157  
  158  
  159  
  160  
  161  
  162  
  163  
  164  
  165  
  166  
  167  
  168  
  169  
  170  
  171  
  172  
  173  
  174  
  175  
  176  
  177  
  178  
  179  
  180  
  181  
  182  
  183  
  184  
  185  
  186                                 -3-
  187

  192  
  193  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
  194  
  195  
  196  2.  System Description
  197  
  198  
  199  
  200       Figure 2-1 illustrates the design of the TCP system, showing
  201  
  202  all of the interactions involved when  two  user  processes  each
  203  
  204  have a single open connection.
  205  
  206  
  207       The  TCP  process  is the center of activity in this design.
  208  
  209  It  interfaces  to  the  network  via  the  UNIX  device  driver,
  210  
  211  implements  the  TCP  protocol,  and interfaces to user processes
  212  
  213  using UNIX ports as communication links.
  214  
  215  
  216          The TCP process is structured in two  modules,  the  core
  217  
  218  and  the  driver.   The  core module is a derivative of the TCP11
  219  
  220  program [2], modified for the UNIX  environment  and  to  support
  221  
  222  multiple  connections.  It implements the actual machinery of the
  223  
  224  TCP, but  interfaces  to  the  real  time  clock,  network,  user
  225  
  226  processes, and storage manager, via the driver module.
  227  
  228  
  229          The  driver  module  implements the interface protocol to
  230  
  231  user processes, and contains  a  task  scheduler  to  direct  the
  232  
  233  core's  attention  to various connections in turn for processing.
  234  
  235  It acts essentially as a scheduler, allocating the  resources  of
  236  
  237  the  core  among  the active connections, demultiplexing incoming
  238  
  239  network  traffic,  and  performing  general  resource  management
  240  
  241  within the TCP process.
  242  
  243  
  244  
  245  
  246  
  247  
  248                                 -4-
  249  
  250  
  251  
  252  
  253  
  254  
  255  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
  256  
  257  
  258  
  259  
  260  
  261  
  262  
  263  
  264  
  265  
  266  
  267  
  268  
  269  
  270  
  271  
  272  
  273  
  274  
  275  
  276  
  277  
  278  
  279  
  280  
  281  
  282

  285

  319  
  320  2.1  Interactions Between the TCP and User Processes
  321  
  322  
  323       User  processes  access  the  TCP  facilities by use of UNIX
  324  
  325  ports to communicate with  the  TCP  process.   The  TCP  process
  326  
  327  maintains  a  single  port,  called the TCP ear, which is used to
  328                                          ___ ___
  329  
  330  initiate interactions between a user and the TCP.
  331  
  332  
  333       All communication between the processes is handled in  terms
  334  
  335  of  messages,  which  are  simply  packages of information passed
  336  
  337  between the user  and  TCP  processes.   These  packages  have  a
  338  
  339  defined  format,  which  specifies the identity of the sender, an
  340  
  341  op-code, and other information.
  342  
  343  
  344       A user process sends a message to the TCP ear to inform  the
  345  
  346  TCP  process  of  its  desire  to  use  the TCP facilities.  This
  347  
  348  message initiates the establishment of a  pair  of  private  UNIX
  349  
  350  ports between the TCP and the user which sent the message.  These
  351  
  352  ports  are termed the command and response ports, and are used to
  353                        _______     ________
  354  
  355  convey commands from the user to the TCP process,  and  responses
  356  
  357  from  the  TCP  process  to the user.  Commands and responses are
  358  
  359  simply messages whose op-codes identify them as such.
  360  
  361  
  362  
  363       One  command  is  used  to  request  that  a  specific   TCP
  364  
  365  connection  be  opened.   This  initiates the establishment of an
  366  
  367  additional pair of ports between the user and  TCP  processes  to
  368  
  369  carry  the  data  for  that  connection.   A  pair  of  ports  is
  370  
  371  
  372  
  373  
  374                                 -6-
  375  
  376  
  377  
  378  
  379  
  380  
  381  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
  382  
  383  
  384  established for each connection opened, and serves  as  buffering
  385  
  386  for the incoming and outgoing data streams.
  387  
  388  
  389       The  details  of  the protocol used in communication between
  390  
  391  the TCP  process  and  its  users  are  supplied  in  a  separate
  392  
  393  document[4] discussing the implementation.
  394  
  395  
  396  
  397  
  398  
  399  
  400  
  401  
  402  
  403  
  404  
  405  
  406  
  407  
  408  
  409  
  410  
  411  
  412  
  413  
  414  
  415  
  416  
  417  
  418  
  419  
  420  
  421  
  422  
  423  
  424  
  425  
  426  
  427  
  428  
  429  
  430  
  431  
  432  
  433  
  434  
  435  
  436                                 -7-

  443  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
  444  
  445  
  446  3.  TCP LIBRARY ROUTINES
  447  
  448  
  449       One  view of the TCP system  is  defined  by  the facilities
  450  
  451  provided by the TCP library, written in C.  The  library  creates
  452  
  453  one  of  several  possible environments in which  an  application
  454  
  455  program could use TCP.  Other library interfaces could be written
  456  
  457  to  serve  special  needs  as required.
  458  
  459  
  460       This  section describes the functional specifications of the
  461  
  462  library  routines,  which  provide  a  user  interface   to   the
  463  
  464  communication  protocol  used between the TCP and user processes.
  465  
  466  
  467          The library routines in general return a value  which  is
  468  
  469  TRUE  to  indicate  success.  If the requested operation fails, a
  470  
  471  FALSE  (i.e.,  0)   will   be   returned,   and   the    external
  472  
  473  variable  TCP_error   will   be   set   to  a code indicating the
  474  
  475  reason for the error.  Mnemonics are assigned to each code.   The
  476  
  477  list   of   error mnemonics, their assigned code numbers, and the
  478  
  479  actions which are capable of producing each error are  summarized
  480  
  481  in Appendix C.
  482  
  483  
  484       The errors are classified in five groups, which may overlap.
  485  
  486  These  are  identified  by  a code letter enclosed in brackets in
  487  
  488  each instance.  The groups specify one or more reasons  for  each
  489  
  490  particular error, as follows.
  491  
  492  
  493  
  494  
  495  
  496  
  497  
  498                                 -8-
  499  
  500  
  501  
  502  
  503  
  504  
  505  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
  506  
  507  
  508       If  the communication paths between the user process and the
  509  
  510  TCP process are not functioning properly, errors may be reported.
  511  
  512  These are flagged with the code 'C', and generally imply that the
  513  
  514  file system or the TCP process  is  failing.   The  user  process
  515  
  516  should treat such errors as fatal.
  517  
  518  
  519       Errors  which are flagged with the code 'U' may be generated
  520  
  521  because of a user programming error.
  522  
  523  
  524       Because of the use of dynamic buffers within the TCP library
  525  
  526  routines, there may be temporary shortages.  These will  generate
  527  
  528  errors  flagged with 'I' codes.  The user process may treat these
  529  
  530  as temporary errors, and retry the operation after, for  example,
  531  
  532  reading  data  from  a  different  connection,  which may release
  533  
  534  buffers so that the subsequent try will  succeed.   If  too  many
  535  
  536  connections  are  in  use at the same time, the supply of buffers
  537  
  538  may be inadequate,  which  may  be  remedied  by  recreating  the
  539  
  540  library routines appropriately.
  541  
  542  
  543       The  TCP  process also uses dynamic buffer allocation, which
  544  
  545  may result in generation of errors flagged with 'X' codes.   This
  546  
  547  should be viewed as a potentially temporary error, worth retrying
  548  
  549  after a short wait.
  550  
  551  
  552       Finally,   the   library   and   TCP   processes  check  for
  553  
  554  compatibility between themselves, and may generate 'V' errors  if
  555  
  556  
  557  
  558  
  559  
  560                                 -9-
  561  
  562  
  563  
  564  
  565  
  566  
  567  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
  568  
  569  
  570  different  versions attempt to communicate.  The solution to such
  571  
  572  errors is to  make  sure  that  the  installed  versions  of  the
  573  
  574  programs involved are compatible.
  575  
  576  
  577          For each of the library routines,  the  calling  sequence
  578  
  579  and  related  structure  definitions  are  provided,  as  well as
  580  
  581  enumeration of possible errors which may occur.
  582  
  583  
  584       Many of the routines use a timeout parameter,  to  give  the
  585  
  586  user  control of the interactions between the library and the TCP
  587  
  588  process.  The timeout parameter  is  used  only  to  control  the
  589  
  590  length of time permitted in the negotiations with the TCP process
  591  
  592  itself.   The command procedure involves sending a message to the
  593  
  594  TCP process, and waiting for a reply.  Because the TCP process is
  595  
  596  a shared resource, it may be busy, and the reply may be  delayed.
  597  
  598  The  user process can specify a maximum length of time to wait by
  599  
  600  use of the timeout parameter.  Timeouts are primarily intended to
  601  
  602  avoid blocking the user process indefinitely in case of a  system
  603  
  604  malfunction or TCP crash.  User programs should specify a timeout
  605  
  606  large   enough  to  handle  all  normal  delays,  and  treat  any
  607  
  608  occurrence of a timeout as a fatal error.  A typical value  might
  609  
  610  be 15 seconds.
  611  
  612  
  613  
  614  
  615  
  616  
  617  
  618  
  619  
  620  
  621  
  622                                -10-
  623  
  624  
  625  
  626  
  627  
  628  
  629  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
  630  
  631  
  632  3.1  TCP_listen
  633  
  634  
  635          The TCP_listen routine is used to establish contact  with
  636  
  637  the   TCP   process.  It  sets  up the command and response ports
  638  
  639  between the user process and the TCP process, without opening any
  640  
  641  connections.   If  called  while  the  user  process  is  already
  642  
  643  actively  utilizing  the  TCP,  it  acts  as  a  reset   command,
  644  
  645  aborting   any    open    connections    before   attempting   to
  646  
  647  re-establish  contact  with  the TCP process.  TCP_listen must be
  648  
  649  successfully called before any TCP connections may be opened.
  650  
  651  
  652       Calling Sequence:
  653        TCP_Listen(tm);
  654         where:
  655        int tm;         /* timeout, in seconds */
  656  
  657       Function:
  658        Establishes contact between the user process
  659        and the TCP process. If called while TCP activity
  660        is in progress, resets by aborting any open
  661        connections.
  662  
  663       Arguments:
  664        tm -- timeout value, i.e. how long in seconds
  665              TCP_listen should wait for the TCP process
  666              to reply
  667  
  668       Returns:
  669        TRUE if successful, FALSE on error
  670  
  671       Possible Error Codes:
  672          EEARPRT[CXI]  -- open of TCP ear port failed
  673                        -- message send failed
  674          ERSPPRT[CXIV] -- couldn't create response port
  675                        -- error in read from response port
  676                        -- bad response received
  677          ENOBUFS[I]    -- couldn't allocate buffer
  678          ECMDPRT[C]    -- unable to open command port
  679          ETCPNRDY[X]   -- TCP not responding within time specified
  680  
  681  
  682  
  683  
  684                                -11-
  685  
  686  
  687  
  688  
  689  
  690  
  691  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
  692  
  693  
  694  3.2  TCP_quit
  695  
  696  
  697       The inverse operation to  TCP_listen  is  performed  by  the
  698  
  699  TCP_quit  routine.  TCP_quit will cleanly break the communication
  700  
  701  paths to the TCP.  If any connections were still open, they  will
  702  
  703  be  aborted  (see TCP_abort).  This routine should be called when
  704  
  705  TCP activity is complete,  in  order  to  release  the  resources
  706  
  707  allocated  within  both  the  user  process  and the TCP process.
  708  
  709  Killing the user process will have the  same  effect  as  calling
  710  
  711  TCP_quit.
  712  
  713  
  714       Calling sequence:
  715        TCP_quit();
  716  
  717       Function:
  718        Finishes usage of the TCP by the user process.
  719        Releases all resources, and aborts any remaining
  720        connections.
  721  
  722       Arguments:
  723        None
  724  
  725       Returns:
  726        TRUE if successful, else FALSE
  727  
  728       Possible Error Codes:
  729        None
  730  
  731  
  732  
  733  
  734  
  735  
  736  
  737  
  738  
  739  
  740  
  741  
  742  
  743  
  744  
  745  
  746                                -12-
  747  
  748  
  749  
  750  
  751  
  752  
  753  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
  754  
  755  
  756  3.3  TCP_open
  757  
  758  
  759       The  TCP_open  routine  is  used to create a TCP connection.
  760  
  761  The parameters of the connection are specified  by  a  connection
  762                                                         __________
  763  
  764  address  block  structure, or cab.  The contents of the structure
  765  _______  _____                ___
  766  
  767  specify the address of the foreign socket as well  as  the  local
  768  
  769  address  components  which  the  user is permitted to specify.  A
  770  
  771  mask is also supplied to specify which events  will  trigger  the
  772  
  773  issuance  of  change-notices,  as  discussed with the TCP_receive
  774                ______ _______
  775  
  776  specifications.
  777  
  778  
  779       If the connection is accepted by the TCP  process,  TCP_open
  780  
  781  will  return  a pointer to a structure termed a user transmission
  782                                                  ____ ____________
  783  
  784  control block, or utcb, which is used to identify the  connection
  785  _______ _____     ____
  786  
  787  for   other   routine   calls.   The  connection  itself  is  not
  788  
  789  necessarily 'established' in the TCP sense,  since  the  call  to
  790  
  791  TCP_open  returns  before  the  TCP  process begins the handshake
  792  
  793  procedure with the foreign TCP.
  794  
  795  
  796          For partially specified connections, the TCP   does   not
  797  
  798  begin  the handshake until an acceptable message arrives from the
  799  
  800  foreign  site.   For   fully   specified   connections,  the  TCP
  801  
  802  will normally  begin  the  handshake  procedure   by  sending  an
  803  
  804  initial  message  to  the specified foreign socket.  This may  be
  805  
  806  suppressed by  specifying  the connection to  be  listen-only  as
  807  
  808  described  below.   The TCP will not initiate a handshake in this
  809  
  810  
  811  
  812  
  813                                -13-
  814  
  815  
  816  
  817  
  818  
  819  
  820  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
  821  
  822  
  823  case, but  will,  of  course,  respond  to  an  incoming  message
  824  
  825  directed to the connection.  If the user process attempts to send
  826  
  827  data  on  a  listen-only  connection  which  has  not  yet become
  828  
  829  established, the TCP will change the connection type  to  normal,
  830  
  831  and initiate the handshake procedure at that time.
  832  
  833  
  834  
  835       Calling Sequence:
  836        pt=TCP_Open(c, tm, f)
  837         where:
  838        struct cab *c ;         /* specifies connection */
  839        int tm ;                /* in seconds */
  840        char f ;                /* mode of opening */
  841        struct utcb *pt ;       /* if successful */
  842  
  843       Function:
  844        Creates a TCP connection, obtaining a pointer
  845        to a utcb to be used for subsequent data transfers.
  846        Optionally begins establishment of the connection
  847        with the foreign TCP.
  848  
  849       Arguments:
  850          c  -- a cab structure, as defined below
  851          tm -- time to allow for the TCP to reply to the
  852                open request
  853          f  -- mode bits.  Assigned bits are:
  854                01 -- open as a listen-only connection
  855  
  856          Returns:
  857           TRUE, pointer to utcb, FALSE on error
  858  
  859          Possible Error Codes:
  860          ETCPNRDY[X]   -- TCP took too long to respond
  861          ECMDPRT[C}    -- error in use of command-port
  862          ERSPPRT[CV]   -- error in use of response-port
  863          ETCPBAD[V]    -- illegal message from TCP process
  864                           (not conforming to the protocol)
  865          EUNKR[V]      -- unknown type of message from TCP
  866          ENOBUFS[IX}   -- either the user process or TCP
  867                           process is short of buffer space
  868          ESNDPRT[CIX]  -- couldn't open send-port
  869          ERCVPRT[CIXV] -- couldn't open receive-port
  870                        -- error in reading from receive-port
  871  
  872  
  873  
  874  
  875                                -14-
  876  
  877  
  878  
  879  
  880  
  881  
  882  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
  883  
  884  
  885                        -- message received violates protocol
  886          EUSRC[I]      -- too many connections in this process
  887          ESYSC[X]      -- too many connections system-wide
  888          ESYSF[X]      -- too many processes using TCP
  889          EINUS[X]      -- connection already open by someone
  890          EILLLS[U]     -- illegal port specification
  891  
  892  
  893       The   connection   to  be  opened  is  specified  by  a  cab
  894  
  895  (connection address block) structure, defined as follows.
  896  
  897  
  898  struct  cab {
  899          char    c_lph ;    /* local port, high byte */
  900          char    c_xx1 ;    /* used internally */
  901          int     c_lpm ;    /* local port, low 2 bytes */
  902          char    c_fnid ;   /* destination net */
  903          char    c_ftidh ;  /* foreign TCP, high byte */
  904          int     c_ftid ;   /* destination host (low 2 bytes) */
  905          char    c_fph ;    /* destination high byte */
  906          char    c_xx2 ;    /* used internally */
  907          int     c_fpm ;    /* destination port (low bytes) */
  908          int     c_cnmask ; /* change-notice mask */
  909  };
  910  
  911  
  912       The elements specified as used internally will  be  used  by
  913  
  914  the TCP_open routine for temporary storage.
  915  
  916  
  917       The foreign net, host, or port, or some combination of these
  918  
  919  may   be  unspecified,  by  supplying  zero  value(s)  for  those
  920  
  921  elements.  The following combinations are permitted:
  922  
  923  
  924                          Net    Host    Port
  925                          ___________________
  926  
  927                           S       S       S
  928                           S       S       U
  929                           S       U       U
  930                           U       U       U
  931  
  932       Where S = Specified, U = Unspecified.
  933  
  934  
  935  
  936  
  937  
  938                                -15-
  939  
  940  
  941  
  942  
  943  
  944  
  945  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
  946  
  947  
  948          A connection with any unspecified foreign  parameters  is
  949  
  950  inherently  open for 'listening'.  Any data sent by the  user  on
  951  
  952  such a connection will be queued for sending by the TCP, and will
  953  
  954  be  sent  when the connection finally becomes established to some
  955  
  956  specific foreign socket.  When the first message arrives for such
  957  
  958  a connection, it becomes  totally  specified,  by  replacing  any
  959  
  960  previously  unspecified  foreign parameters with those present in
  961  
  962  the message.  The user may determine the values of these, as well
  963  
  964  as other parameters, by use of the TCP_status facility.
  965  
  966  
  967  
  968  
  969  
  970  
  971  
  972  
  973  
  974  
  975  
  976  
  977  
  978  
  979  
  980  
  981  
  982  
  983  
  984  
  985  
  986  
  987  
  988  
  989  
  990  
  991  
  992  
  993  
  994  
  995  
  996  
  997  
  998  
  999  
 1000                                -16-
 1001  
 1002  
 1003  
 1004  
 1005  
 1006  
 1007  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
 1008  
 1009  
 1010  3.4  TCP_close
 1011  
 1012  
 1013       The TCP_close routine is used to initiate a close procedure.
 1014  
 1015  It will cause the TCP process to transmit a close request to  the
 1016  
 1017  foreign  TCP.   The  TCP_close routine will return as soon as the
 1018  
 1019  close command is accepted by the  TCP  process.   The  connection
 1020  
 1021  will  remain  open  until the close procedure has been completed.
 1022  
 1023  The user process may wait for a  change-notice  to  arrive  which
 1024  
 1025  signals the completion of the close procedure.
 1026  
 1027  
 1028       Note that the connection may be used for receiving data even
 1029  
 1030  after  the  close  has been requested, until the connection state
 1031  
 1032  becomes 'closed', by negotiation with the foreign TCP.  Any  data
 1033  
 1034  that  remains  in  the  TCP buffers for transmission when a close
 1035  
 1036  completes is discarded.
 1037  
 1038  
 1039       Calling Sequence:
 1040        TCP_close(pt,tm)
 1041         where
 1042        struct utcb *pt ;       /* from TCP_open */
 1043        int tm ;                /* timeout */
 1044  
 1045       Function:
 1046        Begins the close procedure.
 1047  
 1048       Arguments:
 1049          pt -- pointer returned by TCP_open
 1050          tm -- timeout limit in seconds
 1051  
 1052          Returns:
 1053           TRUE if successful, FALSE on error
 1054  
 1055          Possible Error Codes:
 1056          ETCPNRDY[X}   -- TCP took too long to respond
 1057          ECMDPRT[C}    -- error in use of command-port
 1058  
 1059  
 1060  
 1061  
 1062                                -17-
 1063  
 1064  
 1065  
 1066  
 1067  
 1068  
 1069  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
 1070  
 1071  
 1072          ERSPPRT[CV]   -- error in use of response-port
 1073          ETCPBAD[V]    -- illegal message from TCP process
 1074                           (not conforming to the protocol)
 1075          EUNKR[V]      -- unknown type of message from TCP
 1076          ENOBUFS[X]    -- buffer shortage
 1077  
 1078  
 1079  
 1080       The library routines, as well as  the  TCP  process  itself,
 1081  
 1082  allocate  buffers and table space for each open connection.  This
 1083  
 1084  space is released only when the user calls the TCP_abort routine.
 1085  
 1086  It is important for the user  process  to  follow  all  TCP_close
 1087  
 1088  calls  with  a  call  to  TCP_abort,  even  if  the connection is
 1089  
 1090  reported  to  have  been  'closed'  by  a  change  notice.    The
 1091  
 1092  separation  of the procedure to close the connection from that to
 1093  
 1094  release the storage used by the connection permits user processes
 1095  
 1096  to obtain statistics information by using TCP_status  even  after
 1097  
 1098  the connection is closed.
 1099  
 1100  
 1101  
 1102       The  typical  sequence  of  operations in finishing use of a
 1103  
 1104  particular connection is to call TCP_close to initiate the  close
 1105  
 1106  procedure,   and  wait  for  a  change-notice  (see  TCP_receive)
 1107  
 1108  indicating that the close procedure is complete.  After receiving
 1109  
 1110  this information, TCP_status may optionally be  used  to  collect
 1111  
 1112  statistics,  and  then  the  connection resources are released by
 1113  
 1114  calling TCP_abort.
 1115  
 1116  
 1117  
 1118  
 1119  
 1120  
 1121  
 1122  
 1123  
 1124                                -18-
 1125  
 1126  
 1127  
 1128  
 1129  
 1130  
 1131  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
 1132  
 1133  
 1134  3.5  TCP_abort
 1135  
 1136  
 1137       The TCP_abort routine, if called before  a  close  procedure
 1138  
 1139  has  completed,  serves  to destroy the specified TCP connection,
 1140  
 1141  without waiting for the foreign  TCP  to  acknowledge.   It  also
 1142  
 1143  serves  the  purpose  of cleaning up the tables and buffers which
 1144  
 1145  were allocated to the connection.
 1146  
 1147  
 1148       Calling Sequence:
 1149        TCP_abort(pt,tm)
 1150         where
 1151        struct utcb *pt ;       /* from TCP_open */
 1152        int tm ;                /* timeout */
 1153  
 1154       Function:
 1155        Destroys a TCP connection, and releases all
 1156        resources assigned to the connection.
 1157  
 1158       Arguments:
 1159        pt -- pointer obtained from TCP_open
 1160        tm -- timeout limit in seconds
 1161  
 1162       Returns:
 1163        TRUE if successful, FALSE on error
 1164  
 1165       Possible error Codes:
 1166          ETCPNRDY[X]   -- TCP tooke too long to respond
 1167          ECMDPRT{C}    -- error in use of command-port
 1168          ERSPPRT[CV]   -- error in use of response-port
 1169          ETCPBAD[V]    -- illegal message from TCP process
 1170                           (not conforming to the protocol)
 1171          EUNKR[V]      -- unknown type of message from TCP
 1172          ENOBUFS[X]    -- buffer shortage
 1173  
 1174  
 1175  
 1176  
 1177  
 1178  
 1179  
 1180  
 1181  
 1182  
 1183  
 1184  
 1185  
 1186                                -19-
 1187  
 1188  
 1189  
 1190  
 1191  
 1192  
 1193  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
 1194  
 1195  
 1196  3.6  TCP_send
 1197  
 1198  
 1199       The TCP_send routine  is  used  to  send  data  over  a  TCP
 1200  
 1201  connection.   It  requires as an argument a pointer to a utcb, as
 1202  
 1203  returned  by  TCP_open.   Data  given  to  TCP_send  is  buffered
 1204  
 1205  internally  for  transmission  to  the  TCP  process.  Therefore,
 1206  
 1207  TCP_send will actually do I/O to transfer data to the TCP process
 1208  
 1209  only when the internal buffer fills, or when it is instructed  to
 1210  
 1211  send  immediately.   Large  data  transfers  will  be  split into
 1212  
 1213  smaller chunks for transfer to the TCP process.   The  TCP_status
 1214  
 1215  routine  can  be used to obtain information concerning the buffer
 1216  
 1217  sizes being used.
 1218  
 1219  
 1220       Two switch parameters to the TCP_send routine  are  used  to
 1221  
 1222  control  initiation  of  data  transfer.   Either the 'go' or the
 1223  
 1224  'eol'  flag will cause the transfer of the  current  data  buffer
 1225  
 1226  within  the  user  process  even  if the buffer is not full.  The
 1227  
 1228  'eol' flag is also used to indicate that the  last  byte  of  the
 1229  
 1230  supplied data should also be marked as end-of-letter.
 1231  
 1232  
 1233       Calling Sequence:
 1234        TCP_send(pt, b, l, go, eol);
 1235         where
 1236        struct utcb *pt ;  /* from TCP_open */
 1237        char *b ;          /* pointer to data */
 1238        int l ;            /* length of data in bytes */
 1239        int go ;           /* 'go' flag */
 1240        int eol ;          /* 'eol' flag */
 1241  
 1242       Function:
 1243        Send a data stream over a TCP connection.
 1244  
 1245  
 1246  
 1247  
 1248                                -20-
 1249  
 1250  
 1251  
 1252  
 1253  
 1254  
 1255  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
 1256  
 1257  
 1258       Arguments:
 1259          pt  -- pointer from TCP_open
 1260          b   -- pointer to byte stream to be sent
 1261          l   -- number of bytes to send
 1262          go  -- boolean, if true, send immediately
 1263          eol -- boolean, if true, indicate eol
 1264                 (also send immediately)
 1265          Returns:
 1266           TRUE if successful, FALSE on error
 1267  
 1268          Possible error codes:
 1269          ESNDPRT[C]    -- The communication path to the
 1270                           TCP process for this connection
 1271                           is failing.
 1272          ENOBUFS[I]    -- No internal buffers are available
 1273                           within the user process.
 1274  
 1275  
 1276  
 1277       The  flag  arguments  follow  standard  UNIX conventions for
 1278  
 1279  boolean values.  TRUE implies that the flag is set.
 1280  
 1281  
 1282       The routine will return after the data has been written into
 1283  
 1284  the port from  the  user  to  the  TCP  process,  if  that  write
 1285  
 1286  operation  was  necessary.   Note  that  large data transfers may
 1287  
 1288  cause several write operations to occur.
 1289  
 1290  
 1291       If the UNIX port to the TCP process becomes full,  the  user
 1292  
 1293  process  will  be  blocked  until the data being transferred will
 1294  
 1295  fit.  To avoid blocking the user process, if  desired,  calls  to
 1296  
 1297  TCP_check or TCP_status should be made to determine how much data
 1298  
 1299  can be written without blocking, before calling TCP_send.
 1300  
 1301  
 1302  
 1303  
 1304  
 1305  
 1306  
 1307  
 1308  
 1309  
 1310                                -21-
 1311  
 1312  
 1313  
 1314  
 1315  
 1316  
 1317  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
 1318  
 1319  
 1320  3.7  TCP_receive
 1321  
 1322  
 1323       The  TCP_receive  routine  performs the inverse of TCP_send.
 1324  
 1325  The user supplies TCP_receive with a buffer area, into which  the
 1326  
 1327  incoming  data  stream  will be placed.  TCP_receive accepts data
 1328  
 1329  from  the  TCP  process  in  packages,  transfers  it  into   the
 1330  
 1331  user-supplied  area until it is filled, an incoming end-of-letter
 1332  
 1333  is encountered, or no more data is available.
 1334  
 1335  
 1336       In addition to data,  TCP_receive  processes  change-notices
 1337                                                     ______ _______
 1338  
 1339  received  from  the TCP process, and passes these on to the user.
 1340  
 1341  A change notice is simply  a  word  of  data  which  encodes  the
 1342  
 1343  occurrence  of  several possible state changes of the connection,
 1344  
 1345  such       as        connection-established,        network-down,
 1346  
 1347  remote-close-received,  and so on.  The user process may use this
 1348  
 1349  information as desired.  One possible usage would be  to  trigger
 1350  
 1351  calls  to  TCP_status  when  some  change-notice  is received, to
 1352  
 1353  obtain more detailed information.  An enumeration of the possible
 1354  
 1355  change-notices is in Appendix D.  The set of state changes  which
 1356  
 1357  will trigger issuance of a change-notice is specified in the call
 1358  
 1359  to TCP_open for the connection in question.
 1360  
 1361  
 1362  
 1363       When  a user process executes a call to TCP_receive, the TCP
 1364  
 1365  will return some data, a change notice,  or  an  indication  that
 1366  
 1367  zero  data  bytes  were received, if no data is available. In the
 1368  
 1369  
 1370  
 1371  
 1372  
 1373                                -22-
 1374  
 1375  
 1376  
 1377  
 1378  
 1379  
 1380  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
 1381  
 1382  
 1383  first two cases, subsequent calls to  TCP_receive  may  be  made,
 1384  
 1385  until all data and change-notices are exhausted.
 1386  
 1387  
 1388       As  with  TCP_send,  this routine uses an internal buffer to
 1389  
 1390  hold data until the user accepts it.   TCP_check  and  TCP_status
 1391  
 1392  may  be  used  to  determine  the amount of data remaining in the
 1393  
 1394  internal buffer, as well as in the port between the user and  TCP
 1395  
 1396  processes.
 1397  
 1398  
 1399       Note  that,  unlike TCP_send, a call to TCP_receive will not
 1400  
 1401  suspend  the  process  if  no  data  is   available.    In   some
 1402  
 1403  applications,  it may be desirable to suspend execution of a user
 1404  
 1405  process until some data is available.  Because a user process may
 1406  
 1407  have several connections active simultaneously, it  is  necessary
 1408  
 1409  to  be  able  to suspend a process until data is available on any
 1410  
 1411  channel.  To accomplish this, the user process may  directly  use
 1412  
 1413  the  process-control  extensions to UNIX, as discussed in Section
 1414  
 1415  4.3.
 1416  
 1417  
 1418       Calling Sequence:
 1419        cnt=TCP_receive(pt, b, l, eol, flg)
 1420         where
 1421        struct utcb *pt ;  /* from TCP_open */
 1422        char *b ;          /* buffer area */
 1423        int l ;            /* maximum buffer length */
 1424        int *eol ;         /* set to indicate e-o-l */
 1425        int *flg ;         /* set to indicate type */
 1426        int cnt ;          /* bytes received, if flg=0 */
 1427  
 1428       Function:
 1429        Receive a data stream from a TCP connection,
 1430        or a change-notice concerning the connection
 1431  
 1432  
 1433  
 1434  
 1435                                -23-
 1436  
 1437  
 1438  
 1439  
 1440  
 1441  
 1442  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
 1443  
 1444  
 1445        state.
 1446  
 1447       Arguments:
 1448          pt  -- pointer from TCP_open
 1449          b   -- pointer to area in which to place data bytes
 1450          l   -- maximum number of bytes to be transferred
 1451          eol -- pointer to integer, to be set to indicate
 1452                 end-of-letter.
 1453          flg -- pointer to integer, to be set to indicate
 1454                 whether data or a change notice is returned
 1455          cnt -- number of bytes transferred if flg indicates
 1456                 data was received
 1457  
 1458  
 1459       Returns:
 1460        TRUE if something received:
 1461         if flg is FALSE, data was received, cnt is count
 1462            of bytes placed into the user buffer (may be zero)
 1463         if flg is TRUE, a change notice was received, and
 1464            flg is the change-notice value.  No data was
 1465            placed in the buffer.
 1466        FALSE if error
 1467  
 1468       Possible error codes:
 1469        ERCVPRT[C] -- i/o error on port from TCP
 1470        ETCPBAD[V] -- unknown type of message from the TCP
 1471  
 1472  
 1473  
 1474  
 1475  
 1476

 1504  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
 1505  
 1506  
 1507  3.8  TCP_status
 1508  
 1509  
 1510       The TCP_status routine is used to obtain current information
 1511  
 1512  concerning the state of a TCP connection.  It issues a command to
 1513  
 1514  the TCP process requesting information concerning  the  specified
 1515  
 1516  connection,  and  uses  the data obtained with some local data to
 1517  
 1518  fill in a user-supplied cstatus structure.  The user program  may
 1519                          _______
 1520  
 1521  use the information obtained in the cstatus structure as desired.
 1522  
 1523  Generally   this   information  falls  in  two  categories,  that
 1524  
 1525  concerned with the TCP connection itself, and that concerned with
 1526  
 1527  the link  between  the  user  and  TCP  processes.   The  cstatus
 1528  
 1529  structure is defined as follows.
 1530  
 1531  
 1532  struct cstatus {
 1533  
 1534          struct  cab c_cab;  /* for picking up specified
 1535                                 params */
 1536          int c_sndspace ;    /* bytes to write before sending
 1537                                 to the TCP */
 1538          int c_sndblock ;    /* bytes to write before blocking
 1539                                 may happen */
 1540          int c_rcvspace ;    /* bytes to read before reading
 1541                                 from TCP */
 1542          int c_rcvblock ;    /* bytes available (not text)
 1543                                 before blocking */
 1544          int c_sndsct ;      /* various relevant params from
 1545                                 statistics vars */
 1546          int c_nr_pkt ;      /* # packets rcvd */
 1547          int c_pr_dup ;      /* # duplicates rcvd */
 1548          int c_pr_bsy ;      /* # discarded packets */
 1549          int c_pr_dmg ;      /* # damaged packets */
 1550          int c_nr_txt ;      /* # text packets rcvd */
 1551          int c_ns_rtx ;      /* # packets retransmitted */
 1552          int c_sndfds ;      /* file descriptors opened port */
 1553          int c_rcvfds ;      /* file descriptors of receive port */
 1554  };
 1555  
 1556  
 1557  
 1558  
 1559  
 1560                                -25-
 1561  
 1562  
 1563  
 1564  
 1565  
 1566  
 1567  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
 1568  
 1569  
 1570       The 'space' elements in the structure indicate the amount of
 1571  
 1572  buffer  area,  for  sending, or data, for receiving, remaining in
 1573  
 1574  the internal buffers.  The 'block' values represent lower  bounds
 1575  
 1576  on  the amount of buffer area or data remaining in the data ports
 1577  
 1578  between the user and TCP processes.
 1579  
 1580  
 1581       The cstatus structure may contain the cab originally used to
 1582  
 1583  open the connection.  This is used primarily to obtain the actual
 1584  
 1585  values of originally  unspecified  connection  address  elements,
 1586  
 1587  after the connection becomes established.
 1588  
 1589  
 1590       Calling Sequence:
 1591        TCP_status(pt, tm, ps)
 1592         where
 1593        struct utcb *pt ;
 1594        int tm ;
 1595        struct cstatus *ps ;
 1596  
 1597       Function:
 1598        Obtains current status information from the
 1599        TCP process, and fills in the elements of
 1600        the user-supplied cstatus structure.
 1601  
 1602       Arguments:
 1603        pt -- pointer from TCP_open
 1604        tm -- timeout limit in seconds
 1605        ps -- pointer to cstatus structure to be filled in
 1606  
 1607       Returns:
 1608        TRUE if status obtained, FALSE on error
 1609  
 1610       Possible Error Codes:
 1611          ETCPNRDY[X]   -- TCP took too long
 1612          ECMDPRT[C]    -- error in use of command-port
 1613          ERSPPRT[CV]   -- error in use of ressponse-port
 1614                        -- bad message (not an ERR or OK)
 1615                           from TCP
 1616          ETCPBAD[V]    -- illegal message from TCP process
 1617                           (not conforming to the protocol0
 1618  
 1619  
 1620  
 1621  
 1622                                -26-
 1623  
 1624  
 1625  
 1626  
 1627  
 1628  
 1629  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
 1630  
 1631  
 1632          EUNKR[V]      -- unknown type of message from TCP
 1633          ENOBUFS[IX]   -- buffer shortage
 1634  
 1635  
 1636  
 1637       The  statistics elements may be used to monitor performance.
 1638  
 1639  Note that TCP_status may be called after the connection has  been
 1640  
 1641  closed,  but before it is aborted, to determine statistics for an
 1642  
 1643  entire connection lifetime.
 1644

 1693  
 1694  3.9  TCP_check
 1695  
 1696  
 1697       The TCP_check routine is used to obtain updated  information
 1698  
 1699  on  the  capacities  of  the  data ports between the user and TCP
 1700  
 1701  processes, without interacting with the  TCP  process.   It  uses
 1702  
 1703  system  calls  to  determine  the  status  of  the two ports, and
 1704  
 1705  updates the related 'space' and 'block' elements  of  a  supplied
 1706  
 1707  cstatus structure.
 1708  
 1709  
 1710       TCP_check  is intended as a simplified status command, to be
 1711  
 1712  used primarily  for  determining  whether  data  may  be  written
 1713  
 1714  without blocking.
 1715  
 1716  
 1717       Calling Sequence:
 1718        TCP_check(pt, ps)
 1719         where
 1720        struct utcb *pt ;
 1721        struct cstatus *ps ;
 1722  
 1723       Function:
 1724        Places accurate values in the c_sndspace,
 1725        c_sndblock, c_rcvspace, and c_rcvblock
 1726        elements of the supplied cstatus structure.
 1727  
 1728       Arguments:
 1729        pt -- pointer from TCP_open
 1730        ps -- pointer to cstatus structure to be updated
 1731  
 1732       Returns:
 1733        TRUE if information obtained, FALSE on error
 1734  
 1735       Possible Error Codes:
 1736        ESNDPRT[C] -- i/o error
 1737        ERCVPRT[C] -- i/o error
 1738  
 1739  
 1740  
 1741  
 1742  
 1743  
 1744  
 1745  
 1746                                -28-
 1747  
 1748  
 1749  
 1750  
 1751  
 1752  
 1753  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
 1754  
 1755  
 1756  4.  Interprocess Communication Extensions to UNIX
 1757  
 1758  
 1759       Several   changes   have   been   made  to  UNIX  to  permit
 1760  
 1761  implementation of  an  efficient  TCP.   Some  of  these  may  be
 1762  
 1763  directly relevant to the user of the TCP library.
 1764  
 1765  
 1766  5.  Capacity
 1767  
 1768  
 1769       Standard  UNIX  does not permit a user to determine how much
 1770  
 1771  empty space is available before  it  does  a  write  system  call
 1772  
 1773  (similarly  for  a  read).   Standard  UNIX  has been extended to
 1774  
 1775  provide an Empty call so that a process may determine  whether  a
 1776  
 1777  one  byte read would hang or not.  There is no similar call for a
 1778  
 1779  write.  The UNIX interprocess communication extensions provide  a
 1780  
 1781  Capacity call which tells how many bytes are available for a read
 1782  
 1783  if  the  file descriptor is for a file which is open for reading,
 1784  
 1785  and returns the number of bytes which may  be  written  into  the
 1786  
 1787  file wouthout suspending the process.
 1788  
 1789  
 1790       This  facility  is  used by the TCP library routines, and is
 1791  
 1792  not needed at the user level for TCP activity.   The  details  of
 1793  
 1794  the  syntax  and  semantics  of  Capacity  are  presented  in the
 1795  
 1796  interprocess communications document[5].
 1797  
 1798  
 1799  
 1800  
 1801  
 1802  
 1803  
 1804  
 1805  
 1806  
 1807  
 1808                                -29-
 1809  
 1810  
 1811  
 1812  
 1813  
 1814  
 1815  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
 1816  
 1817  
 1818  6.  Await
 1819  
 1820       In standard UNIX, when a user reads from an  empty  pipe  or
 1821  
 1822  port,  or  writes  to a full one, the user's process is suspended
 1823  
 1824  until that read or write is satisfied.
 1825  
 1826  
 1827  Thus, should a user have two or more ports, which may produce  or
 1828  
 1829  accept data in any sequence it cannot dismiss but rather must run
 1830  
 1831  continuously  to  poll these ports.  The capacity call makes such
 1832  
 1833  polling possible.  A  system  call  has  been  implemented  which
 1834  
 1835  causes  a  process  to dismiss until a time, or one of a specific
 1836  
 1837  set of events occurs, such as data  becoming  available  on  some
 1838  
 1839  input port, or space on some output port.
 1840  
 1841  
 1842  
 1843       The  details  of  usage  of  the AWAIT call are defined in a
 1844  
 1845  separate document concerning the UNIX IPC facilities.  Use of the
 1846  
 1847  TCP does not require the AWAIT facility, which is made  available
 1848  
 1849  primarily   to   improve   system   efficiency  by  providing  an
 1850  
 1851  alternative to busy-waiting or polling.
 1852  
 1853  
 1854  7.  Usage of Await and Capacity
 1855  
 1856  
 1857       The TCP process itself requires the system  support  of  the
 1858  
 1859  Await  and  Capacity extensions.  User processes interacting with
 1860  
 1861  the TCP process via the TCP library facilities use  the  Capacity
 1862  
 1863  call,  which  is  executed  by the library routines internally to
 1864  
 1865  detect  the  status  of  the  ports  between  the  user  and  TCP
 1866  
 1867  processes.
 1868  
 1869  
 1870                                -30-
 1871  
 1872  
 1873  
 1874  
 1875  
 1876  
 1877  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
 1878  
 1879  
 1880       The library routines provide no means to wait for any event,
 1881  
 1882  since,  in general, the set of events which may be appropriate to
 1883  
 1884  activate the user process is not available  to  the  TCP  library
 1885  
 1886  routines.   A user process may, howevever, use the Await facility
 1887  
 1888  directly by enabling wakeups on any events of interest,  as  well
 1889  
 1890  as those associated with the TCP activity.
 1891  
 1892  
 1893       The  details  of  usage  of  the Await facility are detailed
 1894  
 1895  elsewhere[5].  To use the facility, the user process must be able
 1896  
 1897  to determine  the  file  descriptors  associated  with  each  TCP
 1898  
 1899  connection.   The  TCP-check  routine  can  be used to obtain the
 1900  
 1901  values, so they can be used to specify when to activate the  user
 1902  
 1903  process,  as  explained in the description of the IPC facilities.
 1904  
 1905  
 1906  
 1907  
 1908  
 1909  
 1910  
 1911  
 1912  
 1913  
 1914  
 1915  
 1916  
 1917

 2003  
 2004  Appendix A - UNIX Port Usage
 2005  
 2006  
 2007       The general specifications of the UNIX  port  mechanism  are
 2008  
 2009  defined  in  a report by the Rand Corporation[3].  For use of the
 2010  
 2011  TCP, the configuration of ports which are utilized is  summarized
 2012  
 2013  here  for reference.  In general, the library routines handle the
 2014  
 2015  details of the usage of UNIX ports for TCP activity.
 2016  
 2017  
 2018       For the TCP usage, ports are generally  used  to  provide  a
 2019  
 2020  unidirectional  data  stream between two processes, i.e., the TCP
 2021  
 2022  and the user processes.  One exception to this rule  is  the  TCP
 2023  
 2024  ear port, which is the only port carrying data from more than one
 2025  
 2026  sender.
 2027  
 2028  
 2029       A port is always established by the receiver, and must be in
 2030  
 2031  existence  before  the  sender can perform an open to write data.
 2032  
 2033  Such ports, by convention in the TCP  design,  are  created  with
 2034  
 2035  specifications  that  the  system (UNIX) prepend a full header to
 2036  
 2037  each message sent.  Ports are created by the intended  receiver's
 2038  
 2039  execution of a PORT system call:
 2040  
 2041  
 2042       port(name,0170222,0) -- C calling sequence
 2043  
 2044                 In  this  call,  the name is a pointer to an ASCIZ
 2045            string name of the port.  The  0170222  argument  is  a
 2046            specific  value  of  the mode argument, which selects a
 2047            full header option on  data  transfers.   The  final  0
 2048            argument  specifies  that  the  port  be opened (by the
 2049            receiver) for reading only.
 2050  
 2051  
 2052  
 2053  
 2054  
 2055  
 2056                                -33-
 2057  
 2058  
 2059  
 2060  
 2061  
 2062  
 2063  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
 2064  
 2065  
 2066       port -- trap 59 is the system call for port
 2067       cmdnam
 2068       170222
 2069  
 2070                 In the  assembly  language  version  of  the  port
 2071            operation,  the  port  call is followed by a pointer to
 2072            the name string, and the mode  word.   It  returns  the
 2073            file  descriptor, to be used for subsequent read calls,
 2074            in R0.
 2075  
 2076  
 2077       The sender process opens a port exactly  as  if  it  were  a
 2078  
 2079  normal  file, using the OPEN system call.  The only difference is
 2080  
 2081  that the open will fail if the receiver has not already  executed
 2082  
 2083  the port call to create the port.
 2084  
 2085  
 2086       In  general,  processes  about to create a port should first
 2087  
 2088  use the system UNLINK call, to destroy any  previous  incarnation
 2089  
 2090  of  the  port.   Because  port names are derived from the process
 2091  
 2092  ids, any such port file which is pre-existing is necessarily from
 2093  
 2094  a now-nonexistent process.
 2095  
 2096  
 2097  
 2098       Some consequences of the port implementation should also  be
 2099  
 2100  noted.   Ports  are  used  as  if they are private unidirectional
 2101  
 2102  paths between a user process and the TCP.   However,  the  access
 2103  
 2104  control  mechanisms  do  not permit such fine-grained control, so
 2105  
 2106  that the ports can, in general, be  opened  for  writing  by  any
 2107  
 2108  process.   Because of the full port header, a receiver can always
 2109  
 2110  determine the identity of the sender of any message, and  discard
 2111  
 2112  spurious messages if any occur.
 2113  
 2114  
 2115  
 2116  
 2117  
 2118                                -34-
 2119  
 2120  
 2121  
 2122  
 2123  
 2124  
 2125  Report No. 3724                      Bolt Beranek and Newman Inc.
 2126
